Um… yeah. I mean, I guess the big thing was my divorce being finalized. Then other nights I’ll just stand in the kitchen and pick at crackers or cereal because making an actual meal for myself feels… pointless.\n\nI know that sounds small, and I work in healthcare, so I probably overthink these things sometimes—well, or underthink them when it’s me—but it’s been going on long enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d use the word depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve been kind of off, I guess. It’s hard because in my work I hear people use that word in a lot of different ways, and then I start comparing myself to everyone else, which probably isn’t helpful.\n\nSo… maybe down sometimes? I don’t know. It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything. I’ve just noticed I’m not quite myself, but I’m not sure how to describe it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the things, or I try to. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because I’ve always loved that—my tomatoes used to be this whole project, honestly, I’d bore people talking about soil and all that—and now I’m just sort of standing there with the hose thinking, okay, done.\n\nAnd book club… I’ll show up, because they’re nice women and I don’t want anyone worrying, but it feels kind of flat. I’ll sit there and listen, and sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even manage to start the book. So yes, I guess I’m enjoying things less than I usually would.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or a major life change happen before this started?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I mean, I guess the big thing was my divorce being finalized. Yeah… I do that. Probably more than I should.

I keep going back over the marriage in my head, like I’m reviewing a case file, which is ridiculous because I tell clients all the time that relationships aren’t that simple, there isn’t one clean cause or one person who “failed.” But then when it’s me, I’ll think, well, I must have missed something obvious, or I must have been bad at some basic part of being married.

And then I get stuck on stupid little details, like conversations we had years ago, or even the way we handled holidays—my sister always did these big Thanksgiving things and I remember thinking we were too tired, too busy, whatever. I don’t know why my brain picks those things. But yes, I blame myself a lot, even if part of me knows it’s more complicated than that.

Before the divorce? I’m trying to think… I mean, I’ve had times where I was in a rut, sure. Who doesn’t, especially working in healthcare, and winter is always weird for me because the garden looks so sad and I start buying seed catalogs like that’s going to fix my whole life.

No, not trying to, no. If anything, that’s part of why I finally made myself come in.

My appetite’s just been… all over the map for months.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F50.9, R63.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, nice to meet you too. I guess what finally made me come in is my eating has been really strange for months. Some days I just don’t bother with dinner, and I’ve lost some weight without trying. Then other nights I’ll just stand in the kitchen and pick at crackers or cereal because making an actual meal for myself feels… pointless.\n\nI know that sounds small, and I work in healthcare, so I probably overthink these things sometimes—well, or underthink them when it’s me—but it’s been going on long enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d use the word depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve been kind of off, I guess. It’s hard because in my work I hear people use that word in a lot of different ways, and then I start comparing myself to everyone else, which probably isn’t helpful.\n\nSo… maybe down sometimes? I don’t know. It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything. I’ve just noticed I’m not quite myself, but I’m not sure how to describe it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the things, or I try to. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because I’ve always loved that—my tomatoes used to be this whole project, honestly, I’d bore people talking about soil and all that—and now I’m just sort of standing there with the hose thinking, okay, done.\n\nAnd book club… I’ll show up, because they’re nice women and I don’t want anyone worrying, but it feels kind of flat. I’ll sit there and listen, and sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even manage to start the book. So yes, I guess I’m enjoying things less than I usually would.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or a major life change happen before this started?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I mean, I guess the big thing was my divorce being finalized. It’s funny, because people act like the final paperwork is just a formality, and in some ways it was—we’d already been separated, and there were all these practical things, bank accounts, who keeps which furniture, the kind of stuff that makes you feel like you’re running some weird administrative project.\n\nBut after it was actually finalized, that’s when I noticed things kind of… shifted. That was about a year and seven months ago now, I think. Around then is when all of this started feeling different.\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks at a time, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most of the day nearly every day,” exactly. That sounds so specific, and I start trying to count it up like I’m doing an intake on myself, which is probably not the point.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where I feel lower than usual, kind of heavy or muted, but whether it’s been two full weeks in that way… I’m not sure. Some days I can sort of get through and seem normal, especially if I’m busy or talking to someone. Then other times it catches up with me. So… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless or blaming yourself a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like I’m reviewing a case file, which is ridiculous because I tell clients all the time that relationships aren’t that simple, there isn’t one clean cause or one person who “failed.” But then when it’s me, I’ll think, well, I must have missed something obvious, or I must have been bad at some basic part of being married.\n\nAnd then I get stuck on stupid little details, like conversations we had years ago, or even the way we handled holidays—my sister always did these big Thanksgiving things and I remember thinking we were too tired, too busy, whatever. I don’t know why my brain picks those things. But yes, I blame myself a lot, even if part of me knows it’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Before this started after the divorce, had you ever gone through a similar period where you lost interest in things and felt low for a couple of weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Before the divorce? I’m trying to think… I mean, I’ve had times where I was in a rut, sure. Who doesn’t, especially working in healthcare, and winter is always weird for me because the garden looks so sad and I start buying seed catalogs like that’s going to fix my whole life.\n\nBut this thing now is more like… I still technically do the things I used to enjoy. I’ll water the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would, and I don’t want people asking too many questions. But it feels kind of flat, like I’m just checking a box. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even get myself started.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t know that I had a stretch quite like that for weeks. Not in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, not trying to, no. If anything, that’s part of why I finally made myself come in.\n\nMy appetite’s just been… all over the map for months. There are stretches where I skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes are a little looser and I haven’t been trying at all. And then other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and just eat crackers or cereal because actually cooking for one person feels so pointless. It’s not like I’m dieting or doing some plan. It’s more like I can’t get myself organized around food.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a way similar to this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not before the divorce, not in this same way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had tired seasons, especially with work, and there were times I didn’t feel like doing much, but now it’s different. I’ll still water the garden or go to book club because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want to be that person who disappears. But it feels flat. Like I’m there, but I’m not really getting anything from it. Sometimes I leave early, or I just never get myself started in the first place.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t remember having that go on for weeks like it is now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F50.9, R63.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, nice to meet you too. I guess what finally made me come in is my eating has been really strange for months. Some days I just don’t bother with dinner, and I’ve lost some weight without trying. Then other nights I’ll just stand in the kitchen and pick at crackers or cereal because making an actual meal for myself feels… pointless.\n\nI know that sounds small, and I work in healthcare, so I probably overthink these things sometimes—well, or underthink them when it’s me—but it’s been going on long enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d use the word depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve been kind of off, I guess. It’s hard because in my work I hear people use that word in a lot of different ways, and then I start comparing myself to everyone else, which probably isn’t helpful.\n\nSo… maybe down sometimes? I don’t know. It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything. I’ve just noticed I’m not quite myself, but I’m not sure how to describe it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the things, or I try to. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because I’ve always loved that—my tomatoes used to be this whole project, honestly, I’d bore people talking about soil and all that—and now I’m just sort of standing there with the hose thinking, okay, done.\n\nAnd book club… I’ll show up, because they’re nice women and I don’t want anyone worrying, but it feels kind of flat. I’ll sit there and listen, and sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even manage to start the book. So yes, I guess I’m enjoying things less than I usually would.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or a major life change happen before this started?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I mean, I guess the big thing was my divorce being finalized. It’s funny, because people act like the final paperwork is just a formality, and in some ways it was—we’d already been separated, and there were all these practical things, bank accounts, who keeps which furniture, the kind of stuff that makes you feel like you’re running some weird administrative project.\n\nBut after it was actually finalized, that’s when I noticed things kind of… shifted. That was about a year and seven months ago now, I think. Around then is when all of this started feeling different.\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks at a time, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most of the day nearly every day,” exactly. That sounds so specific, and I start trying to count it up like I’m doing an intake on myself, which is probably not the point.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where I feel lower than usual, kind of heavy or muted, but whether it’s been two full weeks in that way… I’m not sure. Some days I can sort of get through and seem normal, especially if I’m busy or talking to someone. Then other times it catches up with me. So… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless or blaming yourself a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like I’m reviewing a case file, which is ridiculous because I tell clients all the time that relationships aren’t that simple, there isn’t one clean cause or one person who “failed.” But then when it’s me, I’ll think, well, I must have missed something obvious, or I must have been bad at some basic part of being married.\n\nAnd then I get stuck on stupid little details, like conversations we had years ago, or even the way we handled holidays—my sister always did these big Thanksgiving things and I remember thinking we were too tired, too busy, whatever. I don’t know why my brain picks those things. But yes, I blame myself a lot, even if part of me knows it’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Before this started after the divorce, had you ever gone through a similar period where you lost interest in things and felt low for a couple of weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Before the divorce? I’m trying to think… I mean, I’ve had times where I was in a rut, sure. Who doesn’t, especially working in healthcare, and winter is always weird for me because the garden looks so sad and I start buying seed catalogs like that’s going to fix my whole life.\n\nBut this thing now is more like… I still technically do the things I used to enjoy. I’ll water the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would, and I don’t want people asking too many questions. But it feels kind of flat, like I’m just checking a box. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even get myself started.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t know that I had a stretch quite like that for weeks. Not in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, not trying to, no. If anything, that’s part of why I finally made myself come in.\n\nMy appetite’s just been… all over the map for months. There are stretches where I skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes are a little looser and I haven’t been trying at all. And then other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and just eat crackers or cereal because actually cooking for one person feels so pointless. It’s not like I’m dieting or doing some plan. It’s more like I can’t get myself organized around food.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a way similar to this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not before the divorce, not in this same way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had tired seasons, especially with work, and there were times I didn’t feel like doing much, but now it’s different. I’ll still water the garden or go to book club because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want to be that person who disappears. But it feels flat. Like I’m there, but I’m not really getting anything from it. Sometimes I leave early, or I just never get myself started in the first place.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t remember having that go on for weeks like it is now.\n\nDoctor: Since this started after the divorce, have you felt this way pretty continuously for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It’s been since the divorce was finalized… so, about a year and seven months now, I think. I always have to count it out because the paperwork date and the day he moved out are different, and somehow my brain keeps separate calendars for all of that. But the official divorce was about a year and seven months ago, and that’s when this really started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I don’t know why my brain picks those things. But yes, I blame myself a lot, even if part of me knows it’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Before this started after the divorce, had you ever gone through a similar period where you lost interest in things and felt low for a couple of weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Before the divorce? Yes… I think there have been stretches like that. It’s not that I’m sitting and crying every single day, exactly, but most days I just don’t get much from anything.

Like the garden — I used to really love fussing over it, checking what was coming up, moving things around. Now I’ll water it because if I don’t, everything dies, and that feels wasteful. Or book club, I’ll show up because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want people asking too many questions, but I’m sort of… not there. I’ll leave early sometimes, or I won’t even get myself started.

So yes, for two weeks or more at a time, it’s been like that — going through the motions, but everything feels flat.

But yes, I blame myself a lot, even if part of me knows it’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Before this started after the divorce, had you ever gone through a similar period where you lost interest in things and felt low for a couple of weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Before the divorce? I’m trying to think… I mean, I’ve had times where I was in a rut, sure. Who doesn’t, especially working in healthcare, and winter is always weird for me because the garden looks so sad and I start buying seed catalogs like that’s going to fix my whole life.\n\nBut this thing now is more like… I still technically do the things I used to enjoy. I’ll water the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would, and I don’t want people asking too many questions. But it feels kind of flat, like I’m just checking a box. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even get myself started.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t know that I had a stretch quite like that for weeks. It’s more like I can’t get myself organized around food.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a way similar to this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not before the divorce, not in this same way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had tired seasons, especially with work, and there were times I didn’t feel like doing much, but now it’s different. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… not in a dramatic way, maybe, because I’m still showing up to work. I haven’t stopped going. But I’m not functioning the way I used to.

My notes and documentation pile up, and then I have to stay late to catch up or fix things that I used to just handle during the day without thinking. And then I get annoyed with myself because I know the systems, I teach other people how to stay on top of this kind of thing sometimes, and yet there I am with charts backed up.

At home it’s more like… everything has gotten smaller. I don’t see people as much. I let friendships sort of drift because even answering a text feels like another task. And basic routines — laundry, meals, keeping things orderly — they shrink down to the bare minimum. Like I’m still technically doing life, but it’s all very pared down.

It’s funny, because people act like the final paperwork is just a formality, and in some ways it was—we’d already been separated, and there were all these practical things, bank accounts, who keeps which furniture, the kind of stuff that makes you feel like you’re running some weird administrative project.\n\nBut after it was actually finalized, that’s when I noticed things kind of… shifted. That was about a year and seven months ago now, I think. Around then is when all of this started feeling different.\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks at a time, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most of the day nearly every day,” exactly. That sounds so specific, and I start trying to count it up like I’m doing an intake on myself, which is probably not the point.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where I feel lower than usual, kind of heavy or muted, but whether it’s been two full weeks in that way… I’m not sure. Some days I can sort of get through and seem normal, especially if I’m busy or talking to someone. Then other times it catches up with me. So… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless or blaming yourself a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like I’m reviewing a case file, which is ridiculous because I tell clients all the time that relationships aren’t that simple, there isn’t one clean cause or one person who “failed.” But then when it’s me, I’ll think, well, I must have missed something obvious, or I must have been bad at some basic part of being married.\n\nAnd then I get stuck on stupid little details, like conversations we had years ago, or even the way we handled holidays—my sister always did these big Thanksgiving things and I remember thinking we were too tired, too busy, whatever. I don’t know why my brain picks those things. But yes, I blame myself a lot, even if part of me knows it’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Before this started after the divorce, had you ever gone through a similar period where you lost interest in things and felt low for a couple of weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Before the divorce? I’m trying to think… I mean, I’ve had times where I was in a rut, sure. Who doesn’t, especially working in healthcare, and winter is always weird for me because the garden looks so sad and I start buying seed catalogs like that’s going to fix my whole life.\n\nBut this thing now is more like… I still technically do the things I used to enjoy. I’ll water the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would, and I don’t want people asking too many questions. But it feels kind of flat, like I’m just checking a box. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even get myself started.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t know that I had a stretch quite like that for weeks. Not in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, not trying to, no. If anything, that’s part of why I finally made myself come in.\n\nMy appetite’s just been… all over the map for months. There are stretches where I skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes are a little looser and I haven’t been trying at all. And then other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and just eat crackers or cereal because actually cooking for one person feels so pointless. It’s not like I’m dieting or doing some plan. It’s more like I can’t get myself organized around food.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a way similar to this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not before the divorce, not in this same way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had tired seasons, especially with work, and there were times I didn’t feel like doing much, but now it’s different. I’ll still water the garden or go to book club because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want to be that person who disappears. But it feels flat. Like I’m there, but I’m not really getting anything from it. Sometimes I leave early, or I just never get myself started in the first place.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t remember having that go on for weeks like it is now.\n\nDoctor: Since this started after the divorce, have you felt this way pretty continuously for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It’s been since the divorce was finalized… so, about a year and seven months now, I think. I always have to count it out because the paperwork date and the day he moved out are different, and somehow my brain keeps separate calendars for all of that. But the official divorce was about a year and seven months ago, and that’s when this really started.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not like… I’m going to do something to myself. I don’t have a plan or anything like that.\n\nBut I’ll catch myself thinking it might be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And then I get scared, because in my work, if a client said that to me, I’d take it seriously. I know I would. So I feel strange even saying it out loud, but… yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed or unable to enjoy things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there have been stretches like that. It’s not that I’m sitting and crying every single day, exactly, but most days I just don’t get much from anything.\n\nLike the garden — I used to really love fussing over it, checking what was coming up, moving things around. Now I’ll water it because if I don’t, everything dies, and that feels wasteful. Or book club, I’ll show up because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want people asking too many questions, but I’m sort of… not there. I’ll leave early sometimes, or I won’t even get myself started.\n\nSo yes, for two weeks or more at a time, it’s been like that — going through the motions, but everything feels flat.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it hard for you to function at work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yes… not in a dramatic way, maybe, because I’m still showing up to work. I haven’t stopped going. But I’m not functioning the way I used to.\n\nMy notes and documentation pile up, and then I have to stay late to catch up or fix things that I used to just handle during the day without thinking. And then I get annoyed with myself because I know the systems, I teach other people how to stay on top of this kind of thing sometimes, and yet there I am with charts backed up.\n\nAt home it’s more like… everything has gotten smaller. I don’t see people as much. I let friendships sort of drift because even answering a text feels like another task. And basic routines — laundry, meals, keeping things orderly — they shrink down to the bare minimum. Like I’m still technically doing life, but it’s all very pared down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates":["F32.9","F33.9","F43.21","F50.9","R63.4"],"note":"","is_final":false}

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… even on better days it sort of sneaks in.

I’ll be doing something ordinary, like making coffee or driving somewhere, and then suddenly I’m replaying some conversation from the marriage or wondering if I missed something obvious. Like, how did I not know how to keep it together? And then I tell myself that’s not fair, relationships are more complicated than one person just “failing,” I know that. I’ve said that to other people, many times.

But when it’s me, it feels different. I end up thinking maybe I failed at something very basic, like being a good partner or building a life properly. Then I’ll distract myself with some little task, reorganizing a drawer or answering one email, which is ridiculous because it doesn’t actually answer the question, but it gives me something else to do with my hands. So… yes, it’s still there.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, nice to meet you too. I guess what finally made me come in is my eating has been really strange for months. Some days I just don’t bother with dinner, and I’ve lost some weight without trying. Then other nights I’ll just stand in the kitchen and pick at crackers or cereal because making an actual meal for myself feels… pointless.\n\nI know that sounds small, and I work in healthcare, so I probably overthink these things sometimes—well, or underthink them when it’s me—but it’s been going on long enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d use the word depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve been kind of off, I guess. It’s hard because in my work I hear people use that word in a lot of different ways, and then I start comparing myself to everyone else, which probably isn’t helpful.\n\nSo… maybe down sometimes? I don’t know. It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything. I’ve just noticed I’m not quite myself, but I’m not sure how to describe it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the things, or I try to. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because I’ve always loved that—my tomatoes used to be this whole project, honestly, I’d bore people talking about soil and all that—and now I’m just sort of standing there with the hose thinking, okay, done.\n\nAnd book club… I’ll show up, because they’re nice women and I don’t want anyone worrying, but it feels kind of flat. I’ll sit there and listen, and sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even manage to start the book. So yes, I guess I’m enjoying things less than I usually would.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or a major life change happen before this started?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I mean, I guess the big thing was my divorce being finalized. It’s funny, because people act like the final paperwork is just a formality, and in some ways it was—we’d already been separated, and there were all these practical things, bank accounts, who keeps which furniture, the kind of stuff that makes you feel like you’re running some weird administrative project.\n\nBut after it was actually finalized, that’s when I noticed things kind of… shifted. That was about a year and seven months ago now, I think. Around then is when all of this started feeling different.\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks at a time, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most of the day nearly every day,” exactly. That sounds so specific, and I start trying to count it up like I’m doing an intake on myself, which is probably not the point.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where I feel lower than usual, kind of heavy or muted, but whether it’s been two full weeks in that way… I’m not sure. Some days I can sort of get through and seem normal, especially if I’m busy or talking to someone. Then other times it catches up with me. So… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless or blaming yourself a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like I’m reviewing a case file, which is ridiculous because I tell clients all the time that relationships aren’t that simple, there isn’t one clean cause or one person who “failed.” But then when it’s me, I’ll think, well, I must have missed something obvious, or I must have been bad at some basic part of being married.\n\nAnd then I get stuck on stupid little details, like conversations we had years ago, or even the way we handled holidays—my sister always did these big Thanksgiving things and I remember thinking we were too tired, too busy, whatever. I don’t know why my brain picks those things. But yes, I blame myself a lot, even if part of me knows it’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Before this started after the divorce, had you ever gone through a similar period where you lost interest in things and felt low for a couple of weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Before the divorce? I’m trying to think… I mean, I’ve had times where I was in a rut, sure. Who doesn’t, especially working in healthcare, and winter is always weird for me because the garden looks so sad and I start buying seed catalogs like that’s going to fix my whole life.\n\nBut this thing now is more like… I still technically do the things I used to enjoy. I’ll water the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would, and I don’t want people asking too many questions. But it feels kind of flat, like I’m just checking a box. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even get myself started.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t know that I had a stretch quite like that for weeks. Not in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, not trying to, no. If anything, that’s part of why I finally made myself come in.\n\nMy appetite’s just been… all over the map for months. There are stretches where I skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes are a little looser and I haven’t been trying at all. And then other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and just eat crackers or cereal because actually cooking for one person feels so pointless. It’s not like I’m dieting or doing some plan. It’s more like I can’t get myself organized around food.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a way similar to this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not before the divorce, not in this same way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had tired seasons, especially with work, and there were times I didn’t feel like doing much, but now it’s different. I’ll still water the garden or go to book club because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want to be that person who disappears. But it feels flat. Like I’m there, but I’m not really getting anything from it. Sometimes I leave early, or I just never get myself started in the first place.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t remember having that go on for weeks like it is now.\n\nDoctor: Since this started after the divorce, have you felt this way pretty continuously for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It’s been since the divorce was finalized… so, about a year and seven months now, I think. I always have to count it out because the paperwork date and the day he moved out are different, and somehow my brain keeps separate calendars for all of that. But the official divorce was about a year and seven months ago, and that’s when this really started.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not like… I’m going to do something to myself. I don’t have a plan or anything like that.\n\nBut I’ll catch myself thinking it might be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And then I get scared, because in my work, if a client said that to me, I’d take it seriously. I know I would. So I feel strange even saying it out loud, but… yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed or unable to enjoy things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there have been stretches like that. It’s not that I’m sitting and crying every single day, exactly, but most days I just don’t get much from anything.\n\nLike the garden — I used to really love fussing over it, checking what was coming up, moving things around. Now I’ll water it because if I don’t, everything dies, and that feels wasteful. Or book club, I’ll show up because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want people asking too many questions, but I’m sort of… not there. I’ll leave early sometimes, or I won’t even get myself started.\n\nSo yes, for two weeks or more at a time, it’s been like that — going through the motions, but everything feels flat.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it hard for you to function at work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yes… not in a dramatic way, maybe, because I’m still showing up to work. I haven’t stopped going. But I’m not functioning the way I used to.\n\nMy notes and documentation pile up, and then I have to stay late to catch up or fix things that I used to just handle during the day without thinking. And then I get annoyed with myself because I know the systems, I teach other people how to stay on top of this kind of thing sometimes, and yet there I am with charts backed up.\n\nAt home it’s more like… everything has gotten smaller. I don’t see people as much. I let friendships sort of drift because even answering a text feels like another task. It’s not that I’m sitting and crying every single day, exactly, but most days I just don’t get much from anything.\n\nLike the garden — I used to really love fussing over it, checking what was coming up, moving things around. Now I’ll water it because if I don’t, everything dies, and that feels wasteful. Or book club, I’ll show up because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want people asking too many questions, but I’m sort of… not there. I’ll leave early sometimes, or I won’t even get myself started.\n\nSo yes, for two weeks or more at a time, it’s been like that — going through the motions, but everything feels flat.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it hard for you to function at work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yes… not in a dramatic way, maybe, because I’m still showing up to work. I haven’t stopped going. But I’m not functioning the way I used to.\n\nMy notes and documentation pile up, and then I have to stay late to catch up or fix things that I used to just handle during the day without thinking. And then I get annoyed with myself because I know the systems, I teach other people how to stay on top of this kind of thing sometimes, and yet there I am with charts backed up.\n\nAt home it’s more like… everything has gotten smaller. I don’t see people as much. I let friendships sort of drift because even answering a text feels like another task. And basic routines — laundry, meals, keeping things orderly — they shrink down to the bare minimum. Like I’m still technically doing life, but it’s all very pared down.\n\nDoctor: When you have a better day, do you still find yourself preoccupied with the divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… even on better days it sort of sneaks in.\n\nI’ll be doing something ordinary, like making coffee or driving somewhere, and then suddenly I’m replaying some conversation from the marriage or wondering if I missed something obvious. Like, how did I not know how to keep it together? And then I tell myself that’s not fair, relationships are more complicated than one person just “failing,” I know that. I’ve said that to other people, many times.\n\nBut when it’s me, it feels different. I end up thinking maybe I failed at something very basic, like being a good partner or building a life properly. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not always, no… I mean, reminders can set it off, sure, but a lot of the time it’s just there in the background.

Like I’ll go out to water the garden — which I used to really enjoy, I’d notice every little new shoot and spend way too long rearranging pots — and now I’m mostly doing it so things don’t die. It feels flat. Same with book club. I still go sometimes, because I’ve gone for years and people notice if you disappear, but I’ll sit there sort of half-present, and then leave early, or some weeks I don’t even manage to get myself started.

So I wouldn’t say it only happens when something reminds me of the divorce. It’s more like those things can bring it up, but the loss of interest is there most of the time anyway.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F43.21, F50.9, R63.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, nice to meet you too. I guess what finally made me come in is my eating has been really strange for months. Some days I just don’t bother with dinner, and I’ve lost some weight without trying. Then other nights I’ll just stand in the kitchen and pick at crackers or cereal because making an actual meal for myself feels… pointless.\n\nI know that sounds small, and I work in healthcare, so I probably overthink these things sometimes—well, or underthink them when it’s me—but it’s been going on long enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d use the word depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve been kind of off, I guess. It’s hard because in my work I hear people use that word in a lot of different ways, and then I start comparing myself to everyone else, which probably isn’t helpful.\n\nSo… maybe down sometimes? I don’t know. It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything. I’ve just noticed I’m not quite myself, but I’m not sure how to describe it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the things, or I try to. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because I’ve always loved that—my tomatoes used to be this whole project, honestly, I’d bore people talking about soil and all that—and now I’m just sort of standing there with the hose thinking, okay, done.\n\nAnd book club… I’ll show up, because they’re nice women and I don’t want anyone worrying, but it feels kind of flat. I’ll sit there and listen, and sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even manage to start the book. So yes, I guess I’m enjoying things less than I usually would.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or a major life change happen before this started?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I mean, I guess the big thing was my divorce being finalized. It’s funny, because people act like the final paperwork is just a formality, and in some ways it was—we’d already been separated, and there were all these practical things, bank accounts, who keeps which furniture, the kind of stuff that makes you feel like you’re running some weird administrative project.\n\nBut after it was actually finalized, that’s when I noticed things kind of… shifted. That was about a year and seven months ago now, I think. Around then is when all of this started feeling different.\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks at a time, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most of the day nearly every day,” exactly. That sounds so specific, and I start trying to count it up like I’m doing an intake on myself, which is probably not the point.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where I feel lower than usual, kind of heavy or muted, but whether it’s been two full weeks in that way… I’m not sure. Some days I can sort of get through and seem normal, especially if I’m busy or talking to someone. Then other times it catches up with me. So… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless or blaming yourself a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like I’m reviewing a case file, which is ridiculous because I tell clients all the time that relationships aren’t that simple, there isn’t one clean cause or one person who “failed.” But then when it’s me, I’ll think, well, I must have missed something obvious, or I must have been bad at some basic part of being married.\n\nAnd then I get stuck on stupid little details, like conversations we had years ago, or even the way we handled holidays—my sister always did these big Thanksgiving things and I remember thinking we were too tired, too busy, whatever. I don’t know why my brain picks those things. But yes, I blame myself a lot, even if part of me knows it’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Before this started after the divorce, had you ever gone through a similar period where you lost interest in things and felt low for a couple of weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Before the divorce? I’m trying to think… I mean, I’ve had times where I was in a rut, sure. Who doesn’t, especially working in healthcare, and winter is always weird for me because the garden looks so sad and I start buying seed catalogs like that’s going to fix my whole life.\n\nBut this thing now is more like… I still technically do the things I used to enjoy. I’ll water the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would, and I don’t want people asking too many questions. But it feels kind of flat, like I’m just checking a box. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even get myself started.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t know that I had a stretch quite like that for weeks. Not in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, not trying to, no. If anything, that’s part of why I finally made myself come in.\n\nMy appetite’s just been… all over the map for months. There are stretches where I skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes are a little looser and I haven’t been trying at all. And then other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and just eat crackers or cereal because actually cooking for one person feels so pointless. It’s not like I’m dieting or doing some plan. It’s more like I can’t get myself organized around food.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a way similar to this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not before the divorce, not in this same way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had tired seasons, especially with work, and there were times I didn’t feel like doing much, but now it’s different. I’ll still water the garden or go to book club because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want to be that person who disappears. But it feels flat. Like I’m there, but I’m not really getting anything from it. Sometimes I leave early, or I just never get myself started in the first place.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t remember having that go on for weeks like it is now.\n\nDoctor: Since this started after the divorce, have you felt this way pretty continuously for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It’s been since the divorce was finalized… so, about a year and seven months now, I think. I always have to count it out because the paperwork date and the day he moved out are different, and somehow my brain keeps separate calendars for all of that. But the official divorce was about a year and seven months ago, and that’s when this really started.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not like… I’m going to do something to myself. I don’t have a plan or anything like that.\n\nBut I’ll catch myself thinking it might be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And then I get scared, because in my work, if a client said that to me, I’d take it seriously. I know I would. So I feel strange even saying it out loud, but… yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed or unable to enjoy things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there have been stretches like that. It’s not that I’m sitting and crying every single day, exactly, but most days I just don’t get much from anything.\n\nLike the garden — I used to really love fussing over it, checking what was coming up, moving things around. Now I’ll water it because if I don’t, everything dies, and that feels wasteful. Or book club, I’ll show up because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want people asking too many questions, but I’m sort of… not there. I’m not sure I’d phrase it that clearly, like “most of the day nearly every day.” Some days I can get through and look pretty normal from the outside, and then I’ll realize later I was just sort of… muted the whole time.

So… I guess I’ve felt down a lot during those stretches, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it’s most of the day nearly every day.

Some days I can sort of get through and seem normal, especially if I’m busy or talking to someone. Then other times it catches up with me. I’m trying to think… I mean, I’ve had times where I was in a rut, sure. Who doesn’t, especially working in healthcare, and winter is always weird for me because the garden looks so sad and I start buying seed catalogs like that’s going to fix my whole life.\n\nBut this thing now is more like… I still technically do the things I used to enjoy. I’ll water the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would, and I don’t want people asking too many questions. But it feels kind of flat, like I’m just checking a box. And then other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and just eat crackers or cereal because actually cooking for one person feels so pointless. It’s not like I’m dieting or doing some plan. It’s more like I can’t get myself organized around food.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a way similar to this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not before the divorce, not in this same way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had tired seasons, especially with work, and there were times I didn’t feel like doing much, but now it’s different. I’ll still water the garden or go to book club because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want to be that person who disappears. But it feels flat. I don’t know. It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything. I’ve just noticed I’m not quite myself, but I’m not sure how to describe it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the things, or I try to. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because I’ve always loved that—my tomatoes used to be this whole project, honestly, I’d bore people talking about soil and all that—and now I’m just sort of standing there with the hose thinking, okay, done.\n\nAnd book club… I’ll show up, because they’re nice women and I don’t want anyone worrying, but it feels kind of flat. I’ll sit there and listen, and sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even manage to start the book. So yes, I guess I’m enjoying things less than I usually would.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful or a major life change happen before this started?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I mean, I guess the big thing was my divorce being finalized. It’s funny, because people act like the final paperwork is just a formality, and in some ways it was—we’d already been separated, and there were all these practical things, bank accounts, who keeps which furniture, the kind of stuff that makes you feel like you’re running some weird administrative project.\n\nBut after it was actually finalized, that’s when I noticed things kind of… shifted. That was about a year and seven months ago now, I think. Around then is when all of this started feeling different.\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks at a time, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most of the day nearly every day,” exactly. That sounds so specific, and I start trying to count it up like I’m doing an intake on myself, which is probably not the point.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where I feel lower than usual, kind of heavy or muted, but whether it’s been two full weeks in that way… I’m not sure. Some days I can sort of get through and seem normal, especially if I’m busy or talking to someone. Then other times it catches up with me. So… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless or blaming yourself a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like I’m reviewing a case file, which is ridiculous because I tell clients all the time that relationships aren’t that simple, there isn’t one clean cause or one person who “failed.” But then when it’s me, I’ll think, well, I must have missed something obvious, or I must have been bad at some basic part of being married.\n\nAnd then I get stuck on stupid little details, like conversations we had years ago, or even the way we handled holidays—my sister always did these big Thanksgiving things and I remember thinking we were too tired, too busy, whatever. I don’t know why my brain picks those things. But yes, I blame myself a lot, even if part of me knows it’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Before this started after the divorce, had you ever gone through a similar period where you lost interest in things and felt low for a couple of weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Before the divorce? I’m trying to think… I mean, I’ve had times where I was in a rut, sure. Who doesn’t, especially working in healthcare, and winter is always weird for me because the garden looks so sad and I start buying seed catalogs like that’s going to fix my whole life.\n\nBut this thing now is more like… I still technically do the things I used to enjoy. I’ll water the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would, and I don’t want people asking too many questions. But it feels kind of flat, like I’m just checking a box. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even get myself started.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t know that I had a stretch quite like that for weeks. Not in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, not trying to, no. If anything, that’s part of why I finally made myself come in.\n\nMy appetite’s just been… all over the map for months. There are stretches where I skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes are a little looser and I haven’t been trying at all. And then other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and just eat crackers or cereal because actually cooking for one person feels so pointless. It’s not like I’m dieting or doing some plan. It’s more like I can’t get myself organized around food.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a way similar to this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not before the divorce, not in this same way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had tired seasons, especially with work, and there were times I didn’t feel like doing much, but now it’s different. I’ll still water the garden or go to book club because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want to be that person who disappears. But it feels flat. Like I’m there, but I’m not really getting anything from it. Sometimes I leave early, or I just never get myself started in the first place.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t remember having that go on for weeks like it is now.\n\nDoctor: Since this started after the divorce, have you felt this way pretty continuously for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It’s been since the divorce was finalized… so, about a year and seven months now, I think. I always have to count it out because the paperwork date and the day he moved out are different, and somehow my brain keeps separate calendars for all of that. But the official divorce was about a year and seven months ago, and that’s when this really started.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not like… I’m going to do something to myself. I don’t have a plan or anything like that.\n\nBut I’ll catch myself thinking it might be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And then I get scared, because in my work, if a client said that to me, I’d take it seriously. I know I would. So I feel strange even saying it out loud, but… yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed or unable to enjoy things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there have been stretches like that. It’s not that I’m sitting and crying every single day, exactly, but most days I just don’t get much from anything.\n\nLike the garden — I used to really love fussing over it, checking what was coming up, moving things around. Now I’ll water it because if I don’t, everything dies, and that feels wasteful. Or book club, I’ll show up because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want people asking too many questions, but I’m sort of… not there. I’ll leave early sometimes, or I won’t even get myself started.\n\nSo yes, for two weeks or more at a time, it’s been like that — going through the motions, but everything feels flat.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it hard for you to function at work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yes… not in a dramatic way, maybe, because I’m still showing up to work. I haven’t stopped going. But I’m not functioning the way I used to.\n\nMy notes and documentation pile up, and then I have to stay late to catch up or fix things that I used to just handle during the day without thinking. And then I get annoyed with myself because I know the systems, I teach other people how to stay on top of this kind of thing sometimes, and yet there I am with charts backed up.\n\nAt home it’s more like… everything has gotten smaller. I don’t see people as much. I let friendships sort of drift because even answering a text feels like another task. And basic routines — laundry, meals, keeping things orderly — they shrink down to the bare minimum. Like I’m still technically doing life, but it’s all very pared down.\n\nDoctor: When you have a better day, do you still find yourself preoccupied with the divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… even on better days it sort of sneaks in.\n\nI’ll be doing something ordinary, like making coffee or driving somewhere, and then suddenly I’m replaying some conversation from the marriage or wondering if I missed something obvious. Like, how did I not know how to keep it together? And then I tell myself that’s not fair, relationships are more complicated than one person just “failing,” I know that. I’ve said that to other people, many times.\n\nBut when it’s me, it feels different. I end up thinking maybe I failed at something very basic, like being a good partner or building a life properly. Then I’ll distract myself with some little task, reorganizing a drawer or answering one email, which is ridiculous because it doesn’t actually answer the question, but it gives me something else to do with my hands. So… yes, it’s still there.\n\nDoctor: Do your low mood and loss of interest usually come on when something reminds you of the divorce?\n\nPatient: Not always, no… I mean, reminders can set it off, sure, but a lot of the time it’s just there in the background.\n\nLike I’ll go out to water the garden — which I used to really enjoy, I’d notice every little new shoot and spend way too long rearranging pots — and now I’m mostly doing it so things don’t die. It feels flat. Same with book club. I still go sometimes, because I’ve gone for years and people notice if you disappear, but I’ll sit there sort of half-present, and then leave early, or some weeks I don’t even manage to get myself started.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it only happens when something reminds me of the divorce. It’s more like those things can bring it up, but the loss of interest is there most of the time anyway.\n\nDoctor: During those stretches, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d phrase it that clearly, like “most of the day nearly every day.” Some days I can get through and look pretty normal from the outside, and then I’ll realize later I was just sort of… muted the whole time.\n\nBut I don’t know, it’s hard to track. I’m used to asking clients that kind of question, and it sounds simple when you’re the one asking it, but when it’s your own day, you start parsing it — was I sad, or tired, or just quiet, or was I focused on getting through the next thing? And then suddenly I’m thinking about how we document mood in charts, which is probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve felt down a lot during those stretches, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it’s most of the day nearly every day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… not falling asleep so much, usually. I can get myself into bed at a reasonable hour, which somehow feels like an accomplishment lately.

But then I wake up around four in the morning, and my brain just starts sorting through everything — bills, work notes I forgot to finish, things from the divorce. It’s like that hour when every problem seems more urgent than it probably is. I’ll lie there telling myself not to look at the clock, and then of course I look at the clock.

I’ll water the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would, and I don’t want people asking too many questions. But it feels kind of flat, like I’m just checking a box. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even get myself started.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t know that I had a stretch quite like that for weeks. Not in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, not trying to, no. If anything, that’s part of why I finally made myself come in.\n\nMy appetite’s just been… all over the map for months. There are stretches where I skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes are a little looser and I haven’t been trying at all. And then other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and just eat crackers or cereal because actually cooking for one person feels so pointless. It’s not like I’m dieting or doing some plan. It’s more like I can’t get myself organized around food.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a way similar to this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not before the divorce, not in this same way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had tired seasons, especially with work, and there were times I didn’t feel like doing much, but now it’s different. I’ll still water the garden or go to book club because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want to be that person who disappears. But it feels flat. Like I’m there, but I’m not really getting anything from it. Sometimes I leave early, or I just never get myself started in the first place.\n\nBefore all this, I don’t remember having that go on for weeks like it is now.\n\nDoctor: Since this started after the divorce, have you felt this way pretty continuously for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It’s been since the divorce was finalized… so, about a year and seven months now, I think. I always have to count it out because the paperwork date and the day he moved out are different, and somehow my brain keeps separate calendars for all of that. But the official divorce was about a year and seven months ago, and that’s when this really started.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not like… I’m going to do something to myself. I don’t have a plan or anything like that.\n\nBut I’ll catch myself thinking it might be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And then I get scared, because in my work, if a client said that to me, I’d take it seriously. I know I would. So I feel strange even saying it out loud, but… yes, those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed or unable to enjoy things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there have been stretches like that. It’s not that I’m sitting and crying every single day, exactly, but most days I just don’t get much from anything.\n\nLike the garden — I used to really love fussing over it, checking what was coming up, moving things around. Now I’ll water it because if I don’t, everything dies, and that feels wasteful. Or book club, I’ll show up because it’s on the calendar and I don’t want people asking too many questions, but I’m sort of… not there. I’ll leave early sometimes, or I won’t even get myself started.\n\nSo yes, for two weeks or more at a time, it’s been like that — going through the motions, but everything feels flat.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it hard for you to function at work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yes… not in a dramatic way, maybe, because I’m still showing up to work. I haven’t stopped going. But I’m not functioning the way I used to.\n\nMy notes and documentation pile up, and then I have to stay late to catch up or fix things that I used to just handle during the day without thinking. And then I get annoyed with myself because I know the systems, I teach other people how to stay on top of this kind of thing sometimes, and yet there I am with charts backed up.\n\nAt home it’s more like… everything has gotten smaller. I don’t see people as much. I let friendships sort of drift because even answering a text feels like another task. And basic routines — laundry, meals, keeping things orderly — they shrink down to the bare minimum. Like I’m still technically doing life, but it’s all very pared down.\n\nDoctor: When you have a better day, do you still find yourself preoccupied with the divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… even on better days it sort of sneaks in.\n\nI’ll be doing something ordinary, like making coffee or driving somewhere, and then suddenly I’m replaying some conversation from the marriage or wondering if I missed something obvious. Like, how did I not know how to keep it together? And then I tell myself that’s not fair, relationships are more complicated than one person just “failing,” I know that. I’ve said that to other people, many times.\n\nBut when it’s me, it feels different. I end up thinking maybe I failed at something very basic, like being a good partner or building a life properly. Then I’ll distract myself with some little task, reorganizing a drawer or answering one email, which is ridiculous because it doesn’t actually answer the question, but it gives me something else to do with my hands. So… yes, it’s still there.\n\nDoctor: Do your low mood and loss of interest usually come on when something reminds you of the divorce?\n\nPatient: Not always, no… I mean, reminders can set it off, sure, but a lot of the time it’s just there in the background.\n\nLike I’ll go out to water the garden — which I used to really enjoy, I’d notice every little new shoot and spend way too long rearranging pots — and now I’m mostly doing it so things don’t die. It feels flat. Same with book club. I still go sometimes, because I’ve gone for years and people notice if you disappear, but I’ll sit there sort of half-present, and then leave early, or some weeks I don’t even manage to get myself started.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it only happens when something reminds me of the divorce. It’s more like those things can bring it up, but the loss of interest is there most of the time anyway.\n\nDoctor: During those stretches, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d phrase it that clearly, like “most of the day nearly every day.” Some days I can get through and look pretty normal from the outside, and then I’ll realize later I was just sort of… muted the whole time.\n\nBut I don’t know, it’s hard to track. I’m used to asking clients that kind of question, and it sounds simple when you’re the one asking it, but when it’s your own day, you start parsing it — was I sad, or tired, or just quiet, or was I focused on getting through the next thing? And then suddenly I’m thinking about how we document mood in charts, which is probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve felt down a lot during those stretches, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it’s most of the day nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… not falling asleep so much, usually. I can get myself into bed at a reasonable hour, which somehow feels like an accomplishment lately.\n\nBut then I wake up around four in the morning, and my brain just starts sorting through everything — bills, work notes I forgot to finish, things from the divorce. It’s like that hour when every problem seems more urgent than it probably is. I’ll lie there telling myself not to look at the clock, and then of course I look at the clock.\n\nAnd then I’m just exhausted by the time the alarm goes off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It’s more like those things can bring it up, but the loss of interest is there most of the time anyway.\n\nDoctor: During those stretches, have you felt sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d phrase it that clearly, like “most of the day nearly every day.” Some days I can get through and look pretty normal from the outside, and then I’ll realize later I was just sort of… muted the whole time.\n\nBut I don’t know, it’s hard to track. I’m used to asking clients that kind of question, and it sounds simple when you’re the one asking it, but when it’s your own day, you start parsing it — was I sad, or tired, or just quiet, or was I focused on getting through the next thing? And then suddenly I’m thinking about how we document mood in charts, which is probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve felt down a lot during those stretches, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it’s most of the day nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… not falling asleep so much, usually. I can get myself into bed at a reasonable hour, which somehow feels like an accomplishment lately.\n\nBut then I wake up around four in the morning, and my brain just starts sorting through everything — bills, work notes I forgot to finish, things from the divorce. It’s like that hour when every problem seems more urgent than it probably is. I’ll lie there telling myself not to look at the clock, and then of course I look at the clock.\n\nAnd then I’m just exhausted by the time the alarm goes off.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble concentrating?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / healthcare_social_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Diminished_Thinking\nmanifestation: \"During case reviews I have to reread the same notes several times, and simple decisions like which client call to return first or what to buy for dinner can take me an embarrassing amount of time.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, a bit. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.

During case reviews, I’ll catch myself reading the same note three or four times and still not really taking it in, which is ridiculous because this is work I know how to do. And then I’ll get annoyed with myself, which doesn’t exactly help.

Even small decisions take too long. Like deciding which client call to return first — I’ll sit there overthinking the order instead of just doing it. Or I’ll stop at the store and stand in front of the dinner things like it’s some major life decision when it’s just, you know, pasta or chicken. It’s embarrassing.
